\displaystyle\,\equiv\,-\,\underset{1\leqslant i^{\prime}\leqslant n}{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\sum$\,}}\,C_{j,i^{\prime}}\,x_{i^{\prime}}-\underset{1\leqslant j^{\prime}\leqslant c}{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\sum$\,}}\,D_{j,j^{\prime}}\,F_{j^{\prime}}(x)

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ +\sum_{i=1}^{n}\,\Big(T_{i}\,+\underset{1\leqslant i^{\prime}\leqslant n}{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\sum$\,}}\,A_{i,i^{\prime}}\,x_{i^{\prime}}\,+\underset{1\leqslant j^{\prime}\leqslant c}{\raisebox{1.0pt}{$\sum$\,}}\,B_{i,j^{\prime}}\,F_{j^{\prime}}(x)\Big)\,F_{j,x_{i}}(x)

\scriptstyle{(1\,\leqslant\,j\,\leqslant\,c),}

identically for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
near the origin.

Expanding these
c
equations in power series, we obtain:

0\,\equiv\,\sum_{\kappa=0}^{\infty}\,\sum_{i_{1}+\cdots+i_{n}=\kappa}\,x_{1}^{i_{1}}\cdots x_{n}^{i_{n}}\cdot{\sf E}_{j,i_{1},\dots,i_{n}}^{\sf vf}\Big(J^{1+\kappa}F,\,J^{1+\kappa}G,\,T,\,A,B,C,D\Big).

Thus, the
v
ector
f
ields equations are:

0\,=\,{\sf E}_{j,i_{1},\dots,i_{n}}^{\sf vf}\Big(J^{1+i_{1}+\cdots+i_{n}}F,\,T,\,A,B,C,D\Big)

[formula]

These equations are all linear in
T
,
A
,
B
,
C
,
D
.

Special equations which depend only on
T_{1},\dots,T_{n}

are called
transitivity equations
,
for instance for some indices

\underline{j},\underline{i}_{1},\dots,\underline{i}_{n}
:

0\,\equiv\,\sum_{m=1}^{n}\,T_{m}\,\Big(\Phi_{\underline{j},\underline{i}_{1},\dots,\underline{i}_{n},m}^{\sf vf}\big(J^{1+\underline{i}_{1}+\cdots+\underline{i}_{n}}F\big)\Big),

because when searching for locally homogeneous
structures (our goal), we can deduce that the

n
coefficients of
T_{1},\dots,T_{n}
vanish:

0\,=\,\Phi_{\underline{j},\underline{i}_{1},\dots,\underline{i}_{n},m}^{\sf vf}\big(J^{1+\underline{i}_{1}+\cdots+\underline{i}_{n}}F\big)

[formula]

In all affinely homogeneous models obtained in this memoir,
we apply the following algorithm, proceeding by induction
on the jet order
\kappa\geqslant 1
, playing “ping-pong”
between
0={\sf E}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\bullet}}^{\sf nf}

and
0={\sf E}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\bullet}}^{\sf vf}
.

Step 1.

At order
\kappa-1
, analyze the
v
ector
f
ield
equations
0={\sf E}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\bullet}}^{\sf vf}

to determine all
transitivity equations
,
so as to see which of the order
\kappa
jet coefficients

J_{\ast}^{\kappa}F
are dependent:

J_{\ast,{\sf dep}}^{\kappa}F
.
Solve these
J_{\ast,{\sf dep}}^{\kappa}F
.
Only the remaining independent jets remain:

J_{\ast,{\sf ind}}^{\kappa}F
.

Of all the
Steps 1, 2, 3, 4, 5
,
this
Step 1
is the most
time-consuming, from the computational point of view.
In some branches,

this
Step 1

might happen to be very delicate,
not at all straightforward
.

Step 2.

At the next jet order
\kappa
,
pass to the
n
ormal
f
orm
equations

0={\sf E}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\bullet}}^{\sf nf}
,
insert the transitivity constraints
obtained in
Step 1
—  which are
invariant!  — , simultaneously for the
source
\{u=F(x)\}
and for the
source
\{v=G(y)\}
.
Then some of the isotropy group parameters
which still remain free at this jet order
\kappa
,
namely some of the

a_{{\scriptscriptstyle{\bullet}},{\scriptscriptstyle{\bullet}}}
,

b_{{\scriptscriptstyle{\bullet}},{\scriptscriptstyle{\bullet}}}
,

c_{{\scriptscriptstyle{\bullet}},{\scriptscriptstyle{\bullet}}}
,

d_{{\scriptscriptstyle{\bullet}},{\scriptscriptstyle{\bullet}}}
,
which have not been yet used at orders
\leqslant\kappa-1
,
can happen to be appropriate
to normalize, at order
\kappa
,
some of the coefficients
J_{\ast}^{\kappa}G
,
and then symmetrically by invariancy,
to normalize
in exactly the same way

the corresponding coefficients
J_{\ast}^{\kappa}F
.

In some branches,

this Step 2 might be simply computationally blocked,
due to the nonlinear complexity of the equations

0={\sf E}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\bullet}}^{\sf nf}
.

Step 3.

Provided that
Step 1
and
Step 2

have been achieved correctly, without any oblivion,
determine the
stability
subgroup
G_{\sf stab}^{\kappa-1}\subset G
of affine transformations which stabilizes
not only the normalizations done up to orders

\leqslant\kappa-1
, but also the ones done
in
Step 2
. Then
obtain a certain
linear representation

of
G_{\sf stab}^{\kappa-1}
on the vector space

\mathbb{R}^{\ell_{\kappa}}
of the remaining unnormalized
coefficients
J_{\ast,{\sf ind}}^{\kappa}F
,
with target coefficients
J_{\ast,{\sf ind}}^{\kappa}G
:

\Big(J_{\ast,{\sf ind}}^{\kappa}G\Big)\,:=\,\Big(\Pi(a,b,c,d)\Big)\,\Big(J_{\ast,{\sf ind}}^{\kappa}F\Big),

with a certain explicit
\ell_{\kappa}\times\ell_{\kappa}

matrix
\Pi
depending on the
(currently remaining) isotropy group parameters
(a,b,c,d)\in G_{\sf stab}^{\kappa-1}
. We have no
general proof

that a linear representation necessarily appears,
but in all nodes of all trees treated in this memoir,
we always obtained linear representations,
possibly after corrections of mistakes in

Steps 1
and
2

In some branches,
this
Step 3

might lead to a linear representation
which does not show its invariant subspaces,
hence a search for a change of basis
in
\mathbb{C}^{\ell_{\kappa}}
adapted to the existing
invariant subspaces
must be performed before going further.

Step 4.

Decompose
\mathbb{R}^{\ell_{\kappa}}
into orbits
under
G_{\sf stab}^{\kappa-1}
. Find ‘nice’ transversals

T_{\scriptscriptstyle{\bullet}}^{\kappa}

to all of these orbits.

In some branches,
this
Step 4
cannot be passed
unless the linear representation has been
‘simplified’ in some way,
e.g.

by changing the basis of
\mathbb{R}^{\ell_{\kappa}}
.

Step 5.

Create as many branches as there are orbit transversals

T_{\scriptscriptstyle{\bullet}}^{\kappa}
.
Using the
n
ormal
f
orm equations

0={\sf E}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\bullet}}^{\sf nf}
,
determine all stability subgroups

G_{\sf stab}^{\kappa}\subset G_{\sf stab}^{\kappa-1}
associated with all
transversals
T_{\scriptscriptstyle{\bullet}}^{\kappa}
.
Restrict any further analysis to each one of these

T_{\scriptscriptstyle{\bullet}}^{\kappa}
, that is,
work only with higher jets

J^{\kappa+1}\big|_{T_{\scriptscriptstyle{\bullet}}^{\kappa}}
,

J^{\kappa+2}\big|_{T_{\scriptscriptstyle{\bullet}}^{\kappa}}
,
…,
restricted to these transversals.

Then pass to
Step 1
at order
\kappa
.

The reader
interested in
termination
of the

Steps 1-2-3-4-5
‘algorithm’
can skip the next
Section
12
,
and directly ‘jump’ to
Section
13
.

12
.
A Classification of Affinely Homogeneous

S^{2}\subset\mathbb{C}^{3}

Since this memoir is devoted to higher (co)dimensional
affine homogeneity, it is advisable to explain,
for the well studied
[
78
,
87
,
3
,
30
,
27
,
42
,
132
,
15
,
6
,
18
]

case of surfaces
S^{2}\subset\mathbb{C}^{3}
,
what would be the terminal leaves of a classification
in Lie’s spirit.

Remember that we abandon the search for closed forms
of homogeneous models, and that our goal is to obtain
Lie algebras of vector fields  —  sometimes
‘parametrized’ by a number of absolute invariants

{\text{I}}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\bullet}}
.

The branching tree has 13 terminal leaves, with 1 of them,

2c3b4d
, leading to
a contradiction (no homogeneous model).
In the notation I3P1, I3 means 3-dimensional Isotropy,
and
P1 means
1-dimensional moduli space algebraic variety,
as the number of Parameters  —  often
just
\mathbb{C}^{1}
. Further, P0 means
a single model, and rarely, a finite collection of
individual models, and certainly with zero parameter
(numerical constants only).

Remember the little array of creation
of order 2 transversals:

\begin{array}[]{rccc}{\color[rgb]{0,0.37,0}\bf 1}\,\,\,\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.37,0}\downarrow}&F_{2,0}&F_{1,1}&F_{0,2}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0.37,0}\bf 2a}&0&0&0\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0.37,0}\bf 2b}&1&0&0\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0.37,0}\bf 2c}&0&1&0\end{array}

which can be translated as:

\displaystyle\bf\color[rgb]{0,0.37,0}{{\displaystyle 2a}:}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ u

\displaystyle=\,0+0+{\rm O}_{x,y}(3),

\displaystyle\bf\color[rgb]{0,0.37,0}{{\displaystyle 2b}:}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ u

\displaystyle=\,0+x^{2}+{\rm O}_{x,y}(3),

\displaystyle\bf\color[rgb]{0,0.37,0}{{\displaystyle 2c}:}

\displaystyle\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ u

\displaystyle=\,0+x\,y+{\rm O}_{x,y}(3).

As explained above, to each node of the branching diagram,
there corresponds a linear representation.
Before explaining computational details about
how such linear representations appear in some
of the branches (not in all),
let us list the linear representations, together with the
orbit-transversals they create.
At the final nodes consisting of boxed terminal leaves,
there are only trivial, identity linear representations.
Notation is as in Sections
4

and
6
.

As we already saw in Section
7
,
at the node
2b
, with:

u\,=\,x^{2}+F_{2,1}\,x^{2}\,y+{\rm O}_{x,y}(4)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ v\,=\,q^{2}+G_{2,1}\,p^{2}\,q+{\rm O}_{p,q}(4),

the current reduced group consists of matrices of the form:

\left[\,\begin{array}[]{ccc}a_{1,1}&{\color[rgb]{0.37,0,0}\bf 0}&b_{1}\\
a_{2,1}&a_{2,1}&b_{1}\\
{\color[rgb]{0.37,0,0}\bf 0}&{\color[rgb]{0.37,0,0}\bf 0}&{\color[rgb]{0.37,0,0}a_{1,1}^{2}}\end{array}\,\right],\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ 0\,\neq\,a_{1,1}^{3}\,a_{2,2},

and the linear representation is:

\Big(G_{2,1}\Big)\,=\,\Big(\tfrac{1}{a_{2,2}}\Big)\,\big(F_{2,1}\big).

This means that
F_{2,1}
is a
punctual
relative invariant,

see

[
15
]

for the corresponding differential invariant, denoted
there:

{\text{S}}\,:=\,\frac{F_{xx}\,F_{xxy}-F_{xy}\,F_{xxx}}{F_{xx}^{2}}.

In this case, branch creation is immediate:

\begin{array}[]{rc}{\color[rgb]{0,0.37,0}\bf 2b}\,\,\,\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.37,0}\downarrow}&F_{2,1}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0.37,0}\bf 3a}&0\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0.37,0}\bf 3b}&1\end{array}

In Branch
2b3a
, no group reduction occurs,
while in Branch
2b3b
, after having normalized

G_{2,1}:=1=:F_{2,1}
, it necessarily comes the
group reduction
a_{2,2}:=1
.

Next, at the node
2b3a
, with the order 3
power series coefficients immediately read off from the
above array, an analysis
of the (here not delicate) equations

{\sf E}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\bullet}}^{\sf vf}
shows that
there are no other order 4 monomials
than
F_{4,0}\,x^{4}
:

u\,=\,x^{2}+0+F_{4,0}\,x^{4}+{\rm O}_{x,y}(5)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ v\,=\,q^{2}+0+G_{4,0}\,p^{4}+{\rm O}_{p,q}(5).

In
[
15
]
, this is the branch

{\text{S}}\equiv 0
.

In this branch
2b3a
, the linear representation is:

\Big(G_{4,0}\Big)\,=\,\Big(\tfrac{1}{a_{1,1}^{2}}\Big)\,\big(F_{4,0}\big),

hence branch creation is immediate since we work over
\mathbb{C}
:

\begin{array}[]{rc}{\color[rgb]{0,0.37,0}\bf 2b3a}\,\,\,\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.37,0}\downarrow}&F_{4,0}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0.37,0}\bf 4a}&0\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0.37,0}\bf 4b}&1\end{array}

In
[
15
]

the (Monge) differential invariant corresponding
to
F_{4,0}
is denoted:

{\text{P}}\,:=\,\frac{1}{3}\,\frac{-\,5\,F_{xxx}^{2}+3\,F_{xx}\,F_{xxxx}}{F_{xx}^{2}}.

In Branch
2b3a4a
,
the two differential invariants
0\equiv{\text{S}}\equiv{\text{P}}

vanish identically  —
cf.
the equations we denoted

0\equiv{\text{K}}_{1}\equiv\cdots\equiv{\text{K}}_{t}
in the general
setting. Of course,
this hypothesis imposes consequences
at higher order jet levels in the equations

0={\sf E}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\bullet}}^{\sf vf}
.

In Branch
2b3a4b
, we may normalize

F_{4,0}:=1=:G_{4,0}
, and the equation:

1\,=\,\tfrac{1}{a_{1,1}^{2}}\cdot 1,

imposes the group reduction
a_{1,1}:=1
,
even over
\mathbb{R}
,
because we decided to work modulo the action of a finite
group. In fact, to neglect finite group actions
is ‘justified’ by our goal of obtaining
Lie algebras of vector fields parametrized by
absolute invariants (if present).

It happens that beyond
2b3a4a
and
beyond
2b3a4b
, there are no more group
reductions. This is a reason to declare that the

Steps 1, 2, 3, 4, 5
algorithm stops,

cf.
Section
13

infra
.
Nevertheless,
the equations
0={\sf E}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\bullet}}^{\sf vf}

must be still resolved up to some higher jets
in order to completely determine the
concerned two Lie algebras. A list
of 12 homogeneous models with Lie algebras
appears at the end of the present
Section
12
.

As already mentioned,
in Branch
2b3b
,

G_{2,1}:=1=:F_{2,1}
as
well, and the equation:

1\,=\,\tfrac{1}{a_{2,2}}\cdot 1,

imposes the group reduction
a_{2,2}:=1
.
Then at order 4, after taking account of
the equations
0={\sf E}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\bullet}}^{\sf vf}
, we have:

u\,=\,x^{2}+x^{2}\,y+F_{3,1}\,x^{3}\,y+x^{2}\,y^{2}+{\rm O}_{x,y}(5)\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ \ \ \ \ v\,=\,p^{2}+p^{2}\,q+G_{3,1}\,p^{3}\,q+p^{2}\,q^{2}+{\rm O}_{p,q}(5),

and the linear representation at order 4 is:

\Big(G_{3,1}\Big)\,=\,\Big(\tfrac{1}{a_{1,1}}\Big)\,\Big(F_{3,1}\Big),

leading to the 2 order 4 branches:

\begin{array}[]{rc}{\color[rgb]{0,0.37,0}\bf 2b3b}\,\,\,\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.37,0}\downarrow}&F_{3,1}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0.37,0}\bf 4a}&0\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0.37,0}\bf 4b}&1\end{array}

To this

punctual
relative invariant
F_{3,1}

is associated the explicit:

{\text{W}}\,:=\,\frac{F_{xx}^{2}\,F_{xxxy}-F_{xx}\,F_{xy}\,F_{xxxx}+2\,F_{xy}\,F_{xxx}^{2}-2\,F_{xx}\,F_{xxx}\,F_{xxy}}{(F_{xx})^{2}\,\,\big(F_{xx}\,F_{xxy}-F_{xy}\,F_{xxx}\big)^{2/3}},

which was in
[
15
]
an
absolute

differential invariant for the
special
affine
group
{\sf Saff}(\mathbb{C}^{3})
, and which is here is a
relative

(differential) invariant (with the same explicit expression
in terms of
J_{x,u}^{4}F
) for the (full) affine group
{\sf Aff}(\mathbb{C}^{3})
.

From now on, let us content ourselves with just
listing

the linear representations
together with the associated orbit-transversals
because in Theorem
12.1
, the
(truncated) normal forms
will be written anyway.
It remains to show what happens
at the four nodes
/
branches:

\text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.37,0}\bf 2b3b4a}},\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.37,0}\bf 2c}},\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.37,0}\bf 2c3a}},\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \text{{\color[rgb]{0,0.37,0}\bf 2c3b}}.

In Branch
2b3b4a
, the linear representation is:

\Big(G_{5,0}\Big)\,=\,\Big(\tfrac{1}{a_{1,1}^{3}}\Big)\,\Big(F_{5,0}\Big),

leading to the orbit-transversals (sub-branches):

\begin{array}[]{rc}{\color[rgb]{0,0.37,0}\bf 2b3b4a}\,\,\,\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.37,0}\downarrow}&F_{5,0}\\

, the linear representation is:

, the linear representation is:

leading to:

, the linear representation is:

\left[\begin{array}[]{c}G_{2,2}\\

leading to:

\begin{array}[]{rccc}{\color[rgb]{0,0.37,0}\bf 2c3b}\,\,\,\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.37,0}\downarrow}&F_{2,2}&F_{1,3}&F_{0,4}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0.37,0}\bf 4a}&0&0&0\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0.37,0}\bf 4b}&0&0&1\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0.37,0}\bf 4c}&0&1&F_{0,4}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0.37,0}\bf 4d}&1&F_{1,3}&F_{0,4}\end{array}

In the last two lines,
F_{0,4}
, and
F_{1,3}
,
F_{0,4}

are absolute (punctual) invariants.

To present our classification principles on a known geometric
structure,
let us state a theorem which was
essentially established by
Doubrov-Komrakov-Rabinovich
[
30
]

and by Eastwood-Ezhov
[
42
]
.
The theorem below is less precise
in the sense that we abandon
the search for closed forms,
and we abandon to explore finite group equivalences.

Nevertheless, this theorem has the virtue of specifying
explicitly the way how homogeneous models
depend on the branching tree of punctual invariants,
in a purely algebraic manner, deeply related
with the Lie-Fels-Olver recurrence relations
between differential invariants.

Moreover,
this somewhat restricted approach appears to be
more suitable for generalizations
to higher-dimensional geometric structures,
as
e.g.
the
classification of affinely homogeneous surfaces
S^{2}\subset\mathbb{R}^{4}

achieved in
Sections
17

\to

33
,
for which finding closed forms and
/
or
neutralizing finite equivalences
might be problematic,
especially
in circumstances where much more branches exist,
and much more complicated simply transitive models exist.

Theorem 12.1
.

Up to the action of a finite subgroup of
{\sf Aff}(\mathbb{C}^{3})
,
there are 12 (families of)
affinely homogeneous
model surfaces
S^{2}\subset\mathbb{C}^{3}
,
as represented by the diagram on
p.
12
.
